denote by
\langle\kappa_{i}\rangle
the cyclic subgroup of
\Gamma
generated by
\kappa_{i}
, and let
\varsigma_{i}\in\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
be such that
\varsigma_{i}(\infty)=z_{n_{e}+i}
and
\varsigma_{i}^{-1}\kappa_{i}\varsigma_{i}=\matrixquantity(\lx@physics@matrix 1 & \pm 1 \\ 0 & 1\endlx@physics@matrix)
. Let
E_{i}(z,s)
be the
Eisenstein-Mass series
associated with the cusp
z_{n_{e}+i}
, which is defined as (see section 3 in
Teo_2021
)

E_{i}(z,s)=\sum_{\Gamma\backslash\gamma\in\langle\kappa_{i}\rangle}\imaginary\left(\varsigma_{i}^{-1}\gamma z\right)^{s}.

(49)

The series is absolutely convergent for
\real s>1
, is positive for
s=2
and satisfies the equation

\Delta_{0}E_{i}(z,s)=\frac{1}{4}s(1-s)E_{i}(z,s).

(50)

The inner product

\langle\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\rangle^{\text{cusp}}_{\text{TZ},i}=\iint_{\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)}\mu_{1}(z)\,\overline{\mu_{2}(z)}E_{i}(z,2)\rho(z)\differential[2]{z},\qquad i=1,\dots,n_{p},

(51)

in
\mathcal{H}^{-1,1}(\mathbb{H},\Gamma)
, and the corresponding inner products in all
\mathcal{H}^{-1,1}(\mathbb{H},\Gamma^{\mu})
, determines another Hermitian metric on
\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
which is Kähler for each
i=1,\dots,n_{p}
. The metric
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\text{cusp}}_{\text{TZ}}=\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\text{cusp}}_{\text{TZ},1}+\cdots+\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\text{cusp}}_{\text{TZ},n_{p}}
, called the
cuspidal Takhtajan-Zograf (TZ)
metric, is invariant with respect to the Teichmüller modular group
\operatorname{Mod}(\Gamma)
(see subsection
3.2
). Let

\omega_{\text{TZ},i}^{\text{cusp}}=\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2}\sum_{j,k=1}^{3g-3+n}\langle\mu_{j},\mu_{k}\rangle^{\text{cusp}}_{\text{TZ},i}\hskip 2.84526pt\differential{t_j}\wedge\differential{\bar{t}_k}

(52)

be the symplectic form of the
i
-th cuspidal TZ metric and also define
\omega_{\text{TZ}}^{\text{cusp}}=\omega^{\text{cusp}}_{\text{TZ},1}+\cdots+\omega^{\text{cusp}}_{\text{TZ},n_{p}}
. According to
(
ZT_localindextheorem_1991
, Lemma 2)

\lim_{\imaginary z\to\infty}\imaginary(\varsigma_{i}z)f_{\mu_{j}\bar{\mu}_{k}}(\varsigma_{i}z)=\frac{4}{3}\langle\mu_{j},\mu_{k}\rangle^{\text{cusp}}_{\text{TZ},i},\qquad i=1,\dots,n_{p},

(53)

where
\mu_{j},\mu_{k}\in\mathcal{H}^{-1,1}(\mathbb{H},\Gamma)
and
f_{\mu\overline{\mu^{\prime}}}
was defined in Eq.(
46
), the cuspidal TZ metric pertains to the second variation of the hyperbolic metric on
\mathbb{H}
.

•

Elliptic Takhtajan-Zograf metric
. As discussed in
Montplet2016RiemannRochII
;
ZT_2018
, when the Fuchsian group has
n_{e}>1
elliptic generators
\tau_{1},\dots,\tau_{n_{e}}
, the local index theorem of Takhtajan and Zograf
ZT_localindextheorem_1991
for punctured Riemann surfaces can be generalized to include elliptic fixed points. In this case, the role of Eisenstein-Mass series
E_{i}(z,s)
associated with the cusp
z_{n_{e}+i}
is played by the automorphic Green’s function
G_{0}(z,z_{j};s)
associated with the elliptic fixed point
z_{j}
,
j=1,\dots,n_{e}
. More explicitly, for the elliptic generator
\tau_{j}
of
\Gamma
define

\langle\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\rangle^{\text{ell}}_{\text{TZ},j}=\iint_{\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)}\mu_{1}(z)\,\overline{\mu_{2}(z)}G(z_{j},z)\rho(z)\differential[2]{z},\qquad j=1,\dots,n_{e},

(54)

where
z_{j}
is the fixed-point of
\tau_{j}
, and
G(z,z^{\prime})\equiv G_{0}(z,z^{\prime};2)
is the integral kernel of the resolvent
\left(\Delta_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\right)^{-1}
. It was shown in
Theorem 3
of
ZT_2018
that the metrics
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\text{ell}}_{\text{TZ},j}
are also Kähler. In addition, if we denote by
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\text{ell}}_{\{\mathsf{s}_{m}\}}
the sum over all elliptic TZ metrics
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\text{ell}}_{\text{TZ},j}
associated with the elliptic generators
\tau_{j}
that have the same order of isotropy
m
, we expect
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\text{ell}}_{\{\mathsf{s}_{m}\}}
to be invariant under the action of Teichmüller modular group
\operatorname{Mod}(\Gamma)
. Moreover, we will denote by
\omega^{\text{ell}}_{\text{TZ},j}
, the symplectic
(1,1)
-form

\omega^{\text{ell}}_{\text{TZ},j}=\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2}\sum_{l,k=1}^{3g-3+n}\langle\mu_{l},\mu_{k}\rangle^{\text{ell}}_{\text{TZ},j}\hskip 2.84526pt\differential{t_l}\wedge\differential{\bar{t}_k}.

(55)

Finally, the elliptic TZ metric is also intrinsically related to the second variation of the hyperbolic metric on
\mathbb{H}
: The following result was proven by Takhtajan and Zograf in
(
ZT_2018
, Lemma 1 part (iii))

\lim_{w\to w_{j}}f_{\mu_{l}\bar{\mu}_{k}}\circ J^{-1}(w)=\left\langle\partialderivative{w_l},\partialderivative{w_k}\right\rangle_{\text{TZ},j}^{\text{ell}},\qquad j=1,\dots,n_{e},

(56)

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu_{l},\mu_{k}}\in\mathcal{H}^{-1,1}(\mathbb{H},\Gamma)
and
f_{\mu\overline{\mu^{\prime}}}
was defined in Eq.(
46
).

3.2
Moduli Spaces
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
and
\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}(\boldsymbol{m})

In the previous subsection, we have defined the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
as the space of all equivalence classes of representations
\varrho_{\mu}:\Gamma\to\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
and we have seen that
\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
can be realized as a bounded complex domain in
\mathbb{C}^{3g-3+n}
via the so-called Bers embedding. Let
\operatorname{Aut}_{\ast}(\Gamma)
denote the group of proper automorphisms of
\Gamma
, which carry parabolic elements into parabolic elements and elliptic elements of order
m
into elliptic elements with the same order. The group
\operatorname{Aut}_{\ast}(\Gamma)
acts on
\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
via

\imath(\varrho_{\mu})=\varrho_{\mu}\circ\imath,\qquad\imath\in\operatorname{Aut}_{\ast}(\Gamma).

(57)

That this is well-defined, i.e. that
\imath(\varrho_{\mu})
is equivalent to another representation
\varrho_{\mu_{\imath}}
for some
\mu_{\imath}\in\mathfrak{D}(\Gamma)
, follows from the fact that any automorphism
\imath\in\operatorname{Aut}_{\ast}(\Gamma)
induces a quasi-conformal homeomorphism of
\mathbb{H}
. The group
\operatorname{Inn}(\Gamma)
of inner automorphisms of
\Gamma
obviously acts on
\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
as the identity. Let us remind that the factor group
\operatorname{Mod}(\Gamma):=\operatorname{Aut}_{\ast}(\Gamma)/\operatorname{Inn}(\Gamma)
is called the Teichmüller modular group and acts discretely on
\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
by complex-analytic automorphisms that only change the marking of
\Gamma
. Denote by
\operatorname{Mod}_{0}(\Gamma)
the subgroup of
\operatorname{Mod}(\Gamma)
consisting of
pure mapping classes
— i.e. those fixing the cusps and orbfiold points on
O
pointwise.
35
35

35

The Teichmüller modular group
\operatorname{Mod}(\Gamma)
acting on
\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
can be identified with the orbifold mapping class group
\operatorname{MCG}(O)
acting on
\mathcal{T}_{g,n}(\boldsymbol{m})
. Here,
\operatorname{MCG}(O)
is defined as
\operatorname{Homeo}^{+}(O)/\operatorname{Homeo}_{0}(O)
where
\operatorname{Homeo}^{+}(O)
is the group of orientation preserving homeomorphisms of
O
(in the category of orbifolds), and
\operatorname{Homeo}_{0}(O)
is its identity component.
The full Teichmüller modular group
\operatorname{Mod}(\Gamma)
is related to
\operatorname{Mod}_{0}(\Gamma)
by the short exact sequence

1\to\operatorname{Mod}_{0}(\Gamma)\to\operatorname{Mod}(\Gamma)\to\operatorname{Symm}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{s}}\right)\to 1,

(58)

where
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{s}}\right):=\operatorname{Symm}\left(\mathsf{s}_{2}\right)\times\operatorname{Symm}\left(\mathsf{s}_{3}\right)\times\dotsm\times\operatorname{Symm}\left(\mathsf{s}_{\infty}\right)
denotes a subgroup of
\operatorname{Symm}\left(n\right)
consisting of all permutations that leave the signature type
\boldsymbol{\mathsf{s}}=\{\mathsf{s}_{m}\}_{m\in\hat{\mathbb{N}}^{{}^{>1}}}
invariant
engber1975teichmuller
. Then, the quotient space
\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)/\operatorname{Mod}_{0}(\Gamma)
is isomorphic to the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
of smooth algebraic curves of genus
g
with
n=n_{e}+n_{p}
labeled points. According to (
58
), the Tiechmüller modular group acts on
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
via
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{s}}\right)
and the quotient
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}/\operatorname{Symm}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{s}}\right)
is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}(\boldsymbol{m})
— the true moduli space of orbifold Riemann surfaces with signature
(g;m_{1},\dots,m_{n_{e}};n_{p})
.
36
36

36

When all singular points have the same order of isotropy, the situation will be similar to what has been previously studied by Zograf
Zograf_1990
.
Finally, we remark that both
\mathcal{T}_{g,n}(\boldsymbol{m})
and
\mathfrak{M}_{g,n}(\boldsymbol{m})
depend not on the signature of
O
, but rather on its signature type (see
Bers1975DeformationsAM
for more details).

3.2.1
\mathfrak{M}_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{m})

In the remainder of this subsection, we will focus on the
g=0
case for the sake of simplicity and return to
g>1
Riemann orbisurfaces in the next subsection. A
normalized
orbifold Riemann surfaces with signature
(0;m_{1},\dots,m_{n_{e}};n_{p})
is given by a pair
O=(\mathbb{C}\backslash\{w_{n_{e}+1},\dots,w_{n}\},\mathscr{D})
with
\mathscr{D}=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{e}}(1-\tfrac{1}{m_{i}})\,w_{i}
such that
w_{n-3}
,
w_{n-2}
, and
w_{n}
are at
0
,
1
,
\infty
respectively.
37
37

37

We are assuming that the stratum of conical points
\operatorname{Sing}_{m_{\max}}(O)
with largest order of isotropy
m_{\max}\in\hat{\mathbb{N}}^{{}^{>1}}
, has cardinality of at least three. The analysis in cases where this assumption doesn’t hold requires a change of notation, but the fundamental lessons remain the same.
Accordingly, the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}=\mathscr{F}_{n}\left(\hat{\mathbb{C}}\right)\big/\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
is given by the following domain in
\mathbb{C}^{n-3}

\mathcal{M}_{0,n}=\left\{(w_{1},\dots,w_{n-3})\in\mathbb{C}^{n-3}\Big|w_{i}\neq 0,1\quad\text{and}\quad w_{i}\neq w_{k}\quad\text{for}\quad i\neq k\right\},

(59)

where
\mathscr{F}_{n}\left(\hat{\mathbb{C}}\right)
is the configuration space of
n=n_{e}+n_{p}
labeled distinct points in
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
. We will show that
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
38
38

38

What we have called
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
in this paper, is isomorphic to what has been denoted by
W_{n}
in
Zograf1988ONLE
;
Zograf_1990
and by
\mathcal{Z}_{n}
in
Takhtajan:2001uj
.
is covered (in the complex-analytic sense) by the Teichmüller space of orbifold Riemann surfaces with signature
(0;m_{1},\dots,m_{n_{e}};n_{p})
. This will enable us to express the vector fields
\partialderivative{w_i}
on
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
in terms of sections of the holomorphic tangent bundle of Teichmüller space.

Using Theorem
A.7
, we have
O\cong[\mathbb{H}/\penalty\Gamma]
where
\Gamma
is normalized such that the fixed points of
\kappa_{n-2},\kappa_{n-1},\kappa_{n}
are at
z_{n-2}=0
,
z_{n-1}=1
, and
z_{n}=\infty
respectively. Denote by
\mathbb{H}^{\ast}
the union of
\mathbb{H}
and all parabolic points of
\Gamma
. There is a unique (universal) orbifold covering map
J:\mathbb{H}\to O
with
\operatorname{deck}(J)\cong\Gamma
,
39
39

39

One might correctly want to identify the covering map
J
in this subsection with the covering map
\pi_{\Gamma}
in the commutative diagram (
13
). Only for the reasons that will become clear later, we decided to call the covering map in this subsection with
J
.
which extends to a holomorphic isomorphism
[\mathbb{H}^{\ast}/\penalty\Gamma]\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\hat{O}=(\hat{\mathbb{C}},\hat{\mathscr{D}})
that fixes the points
0,1,\infty
40
40

40

In the literature, the
J
is called
Klien’s Hauptmodul
. Actually, it stands as the sole
\Gamma
-automorphic function on
\mathbb{H}
that exhibits a simple pole at
\infty
and fixes
0
and
1
.
and has the property that
w_{i}=J(z_{i})
for
i=1,\dots,n-3
.
41
41

41

The
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
\hat{\mathscr{D}}
refers to
\mathscr{D}+\sum_{j=n_{e}+1}^{n}w_{j}
.
The function
J
is univalent in any
fundamental domain

\mathcal{F}
for
\Gamma
and has the following expansions near cusps and conical singularities (for more details, see Appendix
C
)

J(z)=\left\{\begin{split}&w_{i}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}J^{(i)}_{k}\left(\frac{z-z_{i}}{z-\bar{z}_{i}}\right)^{km_{i}}\hskip 105.2751pt(i=1,\dots,n_{e}),\hskip 14.22636ptz\to z_{i},\\
&w_{i}+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}J^{(i)}_{k}\exp(-\frac{2 \pi\sqrt{-1}k}{|\delta_{i}|(z-z_{i})})\hskip 44.10185pt(i=n_{e}+1,\dots,n-1),\hskip 5.69046ptz\to z_{i},\\
&\sum_{k=-1}^{\infty}J^{(n)}_{k}\exp(\frac{2 \pi\sqrt{-1}k z}{|\delta_n|})\hskip 128.0374ptz\to z_{n}=\infty.\end{split}\right.

(60)

The first coefficients of the above expansions determine the following smooth positive functions on
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
:

\mathsf{h}_{i}=\left\{\begin{split}&\left|J^{(i)}_{1}\right|^{\frac{2}{m_{i}}}\hskip 36.98866pti=1,\dots,n_{e},\\
&\left|J^{(i)}_{1}\right|^{2}\hskip 44.10185pti=n_{e}+1,\dots,n-1,\\
&\left|J^{(n)}_{-1}\right|^{2}\hskip 42.67912pti=n.\end{split}\right.

(61)

Similar to the case of
\mathcal{M}_{g,n}
discussed at the beginning of this subsection, the symmetric group
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{s}}\right)
acts on
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
to give
\mathfrak{M}_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{m})=\mathcal{M}_{0,n}/\operatorname{Symm}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{s}}\right)
, the moduli space of orbifold Riemann surfaces with signature
(0;m_{1},\dots,m_{n_{e}};n_{p})
. In order to describe the action of
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{s}}\right)
on
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
in more detail, we will make the simplifying assumption that the signature of orbifold Riemann surface
O
is given by
(0;\underbrace{m,\dots,m}_{\mathsf{s}},\underbrace{m^{\prime},\dots,m^{\prime}}_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}})
with
\mathsf{s}\equiv\mathsf{s}_{m}
and
\mathsf{s}^{\prime}\equiv\mathsf{s}_{m^{\prime}}>3
. Let us first focus on ordered
\mathsf{s}^{\prime}
-tuples
(w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}})
with each
w_{i}\in\operatorname{Sing}_{m^{\prime}}
. If none of the points
0,1
and
\infty
are fixed in this set of points, then
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\mathsf{s}^{\prime}\right)
will simply be the group of permutations of
\mathsf{s}^{\prime}
objects. This group is generated by the set of transpositions
\{\sigma_{i,i+1}\}_{i=1}^{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-1}
, whose action only involves interchanging
w_{i}
and
w_{i+1}
for
i\neq\mathsf{s}^{\prime}
, and
w_{1}
and
w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}}
for
i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}
. The situation will be a bit more complicated when the points
0,1
and
\infty
are fixed among these points, namely, we have a sector of the form
\{(w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{s^{\prime}-3},0,1,\infty)\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}}\}
. Then the group
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\mathsf{s}^{\prime}\right)
will be generated by the transpositions
\{\sigma_{i,i+1}\}_{i=1}^{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-1}
and the action of transpositions will be followed by a
\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
transformation to ensure that the last three coordinates in
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
remain
0,1
and
\infty
. If
i<\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3
, the transpositions will not affect the points
0,1
and
\infty
, thus no further action of
\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
will be needed. If
i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3
, then the set of branch points will change to
\{(w_{1},w_{2},\dots,0,w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3},1,\infty)\}
, and we need a transformation that will take
w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}\rightarrow 0,1\rightarrow 1
and
\infty\rightarrow\infty
. This transformation is
\gamma_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3,\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-2}=(w-w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3})/(1-w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3})
. Thus in the end we will arrive at
\{(\frac{w_{1}-w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}}{1-w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}},\dots,\frac{w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-4}-w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}}{1-w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}},\frac{w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}}{w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}-1},0,1,\infty)\}
. Repeating the same procedure for
i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-2
and
i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-1
will yield the transformations,
\gamma_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-2,\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-1}=1-w
and
\gamma_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-1,\mathsf{s}^{\prime}}=w/(w-1)
. Putting all these together, the collective action of
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\mathsf{s}^{\prime}\right)
on
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
, can be expressed as
\sigma_{i,i+1}(w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3},0,1,\infty)=(\tilde{w}_{1},\dots,\tilde{w}_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3},0,1,\infty)
such that

\tilde{w}_{k}=\left\{\begin{split}&w_{k}\qquad(k\neq i,i+1),\qquad&i\leq\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-4,\\
&w_{k+1}\qquad(k=i),&i\leq\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-4,\\
&w_{k-1}\qquad(k=i+1),&i\leq\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-4,\\
&\frac{w-w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}}{1-w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}}\qquad(k\leq\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-4),&i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3,\\
&\frac{w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}}{w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}-1}\qquad(k=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3),&i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3,\\
&1-w_{k}\qquad(k\leq\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3),&i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-2,\\
&\frac{w_{k}}{w_{k}-1}\qquad(k\leq\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3),&i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-1.\end{split}\right.

(62)

As we said, the effect of
\sigma^{\prime}_{i,i+1}\in\operatorname{Symm}\left(\mathsf{s}^{\prime}\right)
on
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
is followed by a
\gamma_{i,i+1}\in\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
on the orbifold Riemann surface
O
with the coordinates
w
. This transformation is actually an isomorphism that takes
O
to another orbifold Riemann surface with the coordinates
\tilde{w}=\gamma_{i,i+1}(w)
. For
i\leq\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-4
,
\gamma_{i,i+1}
is simply the identity map. For the other cases we have
\gamma_{i,i+1}:w\rightarrow\tilde{w}=(w-w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3})/(1-w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}),\>i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3
,
\gamma_{i,i+1}:w\rightarrow\tilde{w}=1-w,\>i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-2
and
\gamma_{i,i+1}:w\rightarrow\tilde{w}=w/(w-1)
for
i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-1
. If one wishes to consider the variation of the objects defined on
X
under the action of
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\mathsf{s}^{\prime}\right)
, one should study the effect of these
\gamma_{i,i+1}
’s on them. Now keeping
0,1
and
\infty
fixed in the
\operatorname{Sing}_{m^{\prime}}
stratum, we consider
ordered
\mathsf{s}
-tuples
(w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{\mathsf{s}})
with
w_{j}\in\operatorname{Sing}_{m}
for all
j=1,\dots,\mathsf{s}
,
\mathsf{s}:=|\operatorname{Sing}_{m}|
, and
m<m^{\prime}
. Here, we no longer make any assumptions about
s
. Again, the group
\operatorname{Symm}\left(s\right)
is generated by the transpositions
\{\sigma_{j,j+1}\}_{j=1}^{\mathsf{s}-1}
and their action simply involves interchanging
w_{j}
and
w_{j+1}
. Thus, the
\gamma_{j,j+1}
defined for this set will all be identity, meaning that
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\mathsf{s}\right)
will not have any non-trivial effect on
O
. Moving forward, we can define the direct product of the two symmetric groups
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\mathsf{s}\right)\times\operatorname{Symm}\left(\mathsf{s}^{\prime}\right)
as ordered pairs
(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}),\sigma\in\operatorname{Symm}\left(\mathsf{s}\right),\sigma^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Symm}\left(\mathsf{s}^{\prime}\right)
. The group operations will then be defined naturally on a pairwise basis, and this group acts on
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
,

\displaystyle\{(w_{1},\dots,w_{\mathsf{s}})\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathsf{s}}\}\times

\displaystyle\{(w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3},0,1,\infty)\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}}\}

\displaystyle=\{(w_{1},\dots,w_{\mathsf{s}},w_{\mathsf{s}+1},w_{\mathsf{s}+2},\dots,w_{\mathsf{s}+\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3},0,1,\infty)\}.

It is now clear that we have chosen to fix
0
,
1
, and
\infty
in the stratum
\operatorname{Sing}_{m^{\prime}}
in order to comply with our convention that branch points are ordered with increasing order of isotropy. In particular, the generators of the direct product group are pairs of transpositions
\{(\sigma_{j,j+1},\sigma^{\prime}_{i,i+1})\}_{j=1,i=1}^{j=s-1,i=s^{\prime}-1}
. The action of these pairs on
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
is defined by their separate action on their corresponding subspace:

\displaystyle(\sigma_{j,j+1},\sigma^{\prime}_{i,i+1})

\displaystyle\big(w_{1},\dots,w_{\mathsf{s}},w_{\mathsf{s}+1},w_{\mathsf{s}+2},\dots,w_{\mathsf{s}+\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3},0,1,\infty\big)

(63)

\displaystyle=\Big(\sigma_{j,j+1}(w_{1},\dots,w_{\mathsf{s}});\sigma^{\prime}_{i,i+1}(w_{\mathsf{s}+1},w_{\mathsf{s}+2},\dots,w_{\mathsf{s}+\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3},0,1,\infty)\Big).

Similar to the cases with the single stratum, these transpositions should be followed by a transformation
\gamma_{j,j+1;i,i+1}\in\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
on the orbifold Riemann surface
O
. For any
j
and
i\leq\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-4
, the corresponding transformation is simply the identity map, and for the cases with
i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3,\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-2,\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-1
and arbitrary
j
we have
\gamma_{j,j+1;i,i+1}
’s that is identical to
\gamma_{i,i+1}
’s defined for the stratum
\operatorname{Sing}_{m^{\prime}}
, with
w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}
replaced by
w_{\mathsf{s}+\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}
. This means that (
63
) can be expressed more explicitly by:
(\sigma_{j,j+1},\sigma^{\prime}_{i,i+1})\big(w_{1},\dots,w_{\mathsf{s}},w_{\mathsf{s}+1},w_{\mathsf{s}+2},\dots,w_{\mathsf{s}+\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3},0,1,\infty\big)=\big(\tilde{w}_{1},\dots,\tilde{w}_{\mathsf{s}},\tilde{w}_{\mathsf{s}+1},\tilde{w}_{\mathsf{s}+2},\dots,\tilde{w}_{\mathsf{s}+\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3},0,1,\infty\big)
such that

\tilde{w}_{k}=\left\{\begin{split}&w_{k}\qquad(k\neq j,j+1,\mathsf{s}+i,\mathsf{s}+i+1),\qquad&i\leq\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-4,\forall j,\\
&w_{k+1}\qquad(k=j\textrm{ or }k=\mathsf{s}+i),&i\leq\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-4,\forall j,\\
&w_{k-1}\qquad(k=j+1\textrm{ or }k=\mathsf{s}+i+1),&i\leq\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-4,\forall j,\\
&\frac{w_{k}-w_{\mathsf{s}+\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}}{1-w_{\mathsf{s}+\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}}\qquad(k\leq\mathsf{s}+\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-4),&i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3,\forall j,\\
&\frac{w_{\mathsf{s}+\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}}{w_{\mathsf{s}+\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}-1}\qquad(k=\mathsf{s}+\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3),&i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3,\forall j,\\
&1-w_{k}\qquad(k\leq\mathsf{s}+\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3),&i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-2,\forall j,\\
&\frac{w_{k}}{w_{k}-1}\qquad(k\leq\mathsf{s}+\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3),&i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-1,\forall j.\end{split}\right.

(64)

It is clear that one can continue developing larger direct products with more strata by doing simple changes to (
64
). It is natural that, similar to the case of the single stratum, one needs to look at the related Möbius transformation when dealing with the variation of objects with respect to the action of
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{s}}\right)
. For the symmetric group
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\mathsf{s}^{\prime}\right)
one can define the 1-cocycle
\{f_{\sigma^{\prime}}\}_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Symm}\left(\mathsf{s}^{\prime}\right)}
. It is defined for the generators by

f_{\sigma^{\prime}_{i,i+1}}=\left\{\begin{split}&1\qquad&i=1,2,\dots,\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-4,\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-2,\\
&(w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}-1)^{h^{\prime}(\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-2)}&i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3,\\
&\prod_{k=1}^{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}(w_{k}-1)^{2h^{\prime}}&i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-1,\\
\end{split}\right.

(65)

where
h^{\prime}/2
is the conformal weight corresponding to
{m^{\prime}}
. Note that if
m^{\prime}\to\infty
, namely the case of punctures, (
65
) will be slightly different:

f_{\sigma^{\prime}_{i,i+1}}=\left\{\begin{split}&1\quad&i=1,2,\dots\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-4,\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-2,\\
&(w_{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}-1)^{(\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-2)}&i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3,\\
&\prod_{k=1}^{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}(w_{k}-1)^{2}&i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-1.\\
\end{split}\right.

(66)

The action of the 1-cocycle
f_{\sigma^{\prime}}
can be extended to a general element in
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\mathsf{s}^{\prime}\right)
by the product rule
f_{\sigma^{\prime}_{1}\sigma^{\prime}_{2}}=(f_{\sigma^{\prime}_{1}}\circ\sigma^{\prime}_{2})f_{\sigma^{\prime}_{2}}
,
\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\sigma^{\prime}_{2}\in\operatorname{Symm}\left(s^{\prime}\right)
. For the group
\operatorname{Symm}\left(s\right)
, the effect of the 1-cocycle on the generators is trivial, namely
f_{\sigma_{j,j+1}}=1
. Finally, for the direct product group
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\mathsf{s}\right)\times\operatorname{Symm}\left(\mathsf{s}^{\prime}\right)
the 1-cocycle will be given for the generators by

f_{(\sigma_{j,j+1},\sigma^{\prime}_{i,i+1})}=\left\{\begin{split}&1\quad&i=1,2,\dots\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-4,\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-2,\forall j\\
&(w_{\mathsf{s}+\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}-1)^{h\mathsf{s}+h^{\prime}(\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-2)}&i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3,\forall j\\
&\prod_{k=1}^{\mathsf{s}}(w_{k}-1)^{2h}\prod_{k=\mathsf{s}+1}^{\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-3}(w_{k}-1)^{2h^{\prime}}&i=\mathsf{s}^{\prime}-1,\forall j,\\
\end{split}\right.

(67)

where
h/2,h^{\prime}/2
are the conformal weights corresponding to
{m,m^{\prime}}
. Thus, we can see that the non-triviality of the 1-cocycle
f
is only due to the fixed points
0,1
and
\infty
, and if none of them are involved in the permutation, the 1-cocycle will be trivial.

Remark 3.3
.

As we will see in Lemma
4.1
, a simple way to determine the 1-cocycles
f
is to calculate the variation of the Liouville action due to the transformation of
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
under the symmetric group. In other words, these 1-cocycles are basically the modular anomaly caused by the non-covariance of the action under the effect of the modular group.

As in
Zograf_1990
;
park2015potentials
, let
\{f_{\eta}\}_{\eta\in\operatorname{Symm}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{s}}\right)}
be the 1-cocycle for
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{s}}\right)
on
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
. These 1-cocycles can be used to define a holomorphic
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle over the moduli space
\mathfrak{M}_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{m})
. To do so, one constructs the trivial bundle
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}\times\mathbb{C}
and defines the action of
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{s}}\right)
on this bundle by

(\boldsymbol{w},\tilde{z})\mapsto(\eta\cdot\boldsymbol{w},f_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{w})\tilde{z}),\qquad\boldsymbol{w}\in\mathcal{M}_{0,n},\,\tilde{z}\in\mathbb{C},\,\eta\in\operatorname{Symm}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{s}}\right).

(68)

Then, the desired holomorphic
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
\lambda_{0,\boldsymbol{m}}=(\mathcal{M}_{0,n}\times\mathbb{C})/\sim
over moduli space
\mathfrak{M}_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{m})=\mathcal{M}_{0,n}/\operatorname{Symm}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{s}}\right)
is defined by the identification
(\boldsymbol{w},\tilde{z})\sim(\eta\cdot\boldsymbol{w},f_{\eta}(\boldsymbol{w})\tilde{z})
for all
\eta\in\operatorname{Symm}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{s}}\right)
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
O
be a closed (i.e.,
n_{p}=0
) orbifold Riemann surface with signature
(0;m_{1},\dots,m_{n})
and fix the last three conical points to be at
0
,
1
, and
\infty
(as always, we assume that these three conical points belong to the same stratum). Define a positive function

\mathsf{H}=\mathsf{h}_{1}^{m_{1}h_{1}}\dotsm\mathsf{h}_{n-1}^{m_{n-1}h_{n-1}}\mathsf{h}_{n}^{-m_{n}h_{n}},

on
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
. Then, the
\mathsf{H}
determines a Hermitian metric in the holomorphic
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
\lambda_{0,\boldsymbol{m}}
over
\mathfrak{M}_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{m})
, where
m_{i}
’s are the branching indices and
h_{i}/2
’s are their corresponding conformal weights.

Proof.

We prove this lemma for the case where we have only two strata of
s
branch points of order
m
and
s^{\prime}
branch points of order
m^{\prime}
. Furthermore, we assume that
s^{\prime}>3
, and the last three conical points in the stratum
\operatorname{Sing}_{m^{\prime}}
(
m^{\prime}>m
) are chosen to be at
0
,
1
,
\infty
. In the end, we shall explain how the rest of the cases can be dealt with similarly. We have
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}=\{(w_{1},\dots,w_{s},w_{s+1},w_{s+2},\dots,w_{s+s^{\prime}-3},0,1)\}
. For simplicity of notation, we can, from now on, denote
s+s^{\prime}=n
in the proof. Each of the
\mathsf{h}_{k}
’s can be viewed as a function on
\mathbb{C}
with the appropriate asymptotics (see (
61
), part 3. in Lemma
C.1
and Remark
C.1
). Accordingly,

\begin{split}\log\mathsf{H}=&hm\sum_{k=1}^{s}\left(-2\log m+2\log 2-\lim_{w\to w_{k}}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1-\frac{1}{m}\right)\log|w-w_{k}|^{2}\right)\right)\\
&+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\sum_{k=s+1}^{n-1}\left(-2\log m^{\prime}+2\log 2-\lim_{w\to w_{k}}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|w-w_{k}|^{2}\right)\right)\\
&-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\left(2\log m^{\prime}-2\log 2+\lim_{w\to\infty}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1+\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|w|^{2}\right)\right).\end{split}

(69)

Now we need to calculate the variation of
\log\mathsf{H}
under the effect of
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{s}}\right)=\operatorname{Symm}\left(s\right)\times\operatorname{Symm}\left(s^{\prime}\right)
. For this, it suffices to look at the effect of the generators
\{(\sigma_{j,j+1},\sigma^{\prime}_{i,i+1})\}_{j=1,i=1}^{j=s-1,i=s^{\prime}-1}
. Considering the background we provided above on the structure of the generators, variation is translated through the effect of the transformation
\gamma_{j,j+1;i,i+1}
:

\displaystyle\Delta\log\mathsf{H}=\log\mathsf{H}[\gamma_{j,j+1;i,i+1}]-\log\mathsf{H}.

(70)

By looking at (
64
), we see that the index
j
does not have any non-trivial effect, and we only have to worry about different values of
i
. For
i<s^{\prime}-3
the isomorphism
\gamma_{j,j+1;i,i+1}
is the identity so we for this cases we have
\Delta\log\mathsf{H}=0
. The non-trivial cases are
i=s^{\prime}-3,i=s^{\prime}-2
and
i=s^{\prime}-1
. In the following, we study them separately:

•

i=n-3
case:

The needed Möbius transformation is given by
\gamma_{j,j+1;i,i+1}=(w-w_{n-3})/(1-w_{n-3})
in this case. For simplicity, we denote this by just
\gamma
. Thus, we write:

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle\log\mathsf{H}=\log\mathsf{H}[\gamma]-\log\mathsf{H}=

(71)

\displaystyle-hm\sum_{k=1}^{s}\left(\lim_{\gamma(w)\to\gamma(w_{k})}\left(\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w))+\left(1-\frac{1}{m}\right)\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(w_{k})|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+hm\sum_{k=1}^{s}\left(\lim_{w\to w_{k}}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1-\frac{1}{m}\right)\log|w-w_{k}|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\sum_{k=s+1}^{n-1}\left(\lim_{\gamma(w)\to\gamma(w_{k})}\left(\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w))+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(w_{k})|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\sum_{k=s+1}^{n-1}\left(\lim_{w\to w_{k}}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|w-w_{k}|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{\gamma(w)\to\gamma(\infty)}\left(\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w))+\left(1+\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|\gamma(w)|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to\infty}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1+\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|w|^{2}\right),

where
\tilde{\varphi}
is the transformed counterpart of
\varphi
through the isomorphism
\gamma
. From the invariance of the hyperbolic metric, these two are related by

\varphi(w)=\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w))+\log\left|\frac{\partial\gamma(w)}{\partial w}\right|^{2}=\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w))-2\log\left|1-w_{n-3}\right|.

(72)

We also have

\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(w_{k})|=\left\{\begin{split}&\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(w_{k})|=\log\left|\frac{w-w_{k}}{1-w_{n-3}}\right|\>\>\>\>\>\>&k\leq n-4,\\
&\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(0)|=\log\left|\frac{w}{1-w_{n-3}}\right|\>\>\>\>\>\>&k=n-3,\\
&\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(w_{n-3})|=\log\left|\frac{w-w_{n-3}}{1-w_{n-3}}\right|\>\>\>\>\>\>&k=n-2,\\
&\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(1)|=\log\left|\frac{w-1}{1-w_{n-3}}\right|\>\>\>\>\>\>&k=n-1.\\
\end{split}\right.

(73)

Note that in finding (
73
), we first included the transposition that exchanges
w_{n-3}
and
w_{n-2}=0
and then included the effect of
\gamma
. Putting (
72
) and (
73
) together we look more closely at (
71
). The terms with
k\leq n-4
in the sum are quite straightforward:

\displaystyle I_{1}=

\displaystyle-hm\sum_{k=1}^{s}\left(\lim_{\gamma(w)\to\gamma(w_{k})}\left(\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w))+\left(1-\frac{1}{m}\right)\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(w_{k})|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+hm\sum_{k=1}^{s}\left(\lim_{w\to w_{k}}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1-\frac{1}{m}\right)\log|w-w_{k}|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\sum_{k=s+1}^{n-4}\left(\lim_{\gamma(w)\to\gamma(w_{k})}\left(\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w))+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(w_{k})|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\sum_{k=s+1}^{n-4}\left(\lim_{w\to w_{k}}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|w-w_{k}|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=-hm\sum_{k=1}^{s}\left(\lim_{w\to w_{k}}\left(2\log\left|1-w_{n-3}\right|+\left(1-\frac{1}{m}\right)\log\left|\frac{w-w_{k}}{1-w_{n-3}}\right|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+hm\sum_{k=1}^{s}\left(\lim_{w\to w_{k}}\left(\left(1-\frac{1}{m}\right)\log|w-w_{k}|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\sum_{k=s+1}^{n-4}\left(\lim_{w\to w_{k}}\left(2\log\left|1-w_{n-3}\right|+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log\left|\frac{w-w_{k}}{1-w_{n-3}}\right|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\sum_{k=s+1}^{n-4}\left(\lim_{w\to w_{k}}\left(\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|w-w_{k}|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=-2\left(hs+h^{\prime}(s^{\prime}-4)\right)\log\left|1-w_{n-3}\right|.

For
k=n-3
, we have:

\displaystyle I_{2}=

\displaystyle-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{\gamma(w)\to\gamma(0)}\left(\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w))+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(0)|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to w_{n-3}}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|w-w_{n-3}|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to 0}\left(\varphi(w)+2\log\left|1-w_{n-3}\right|+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log\left|\frac{w}{1-w_{n-3}}\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to w_{n-3}}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|w-w_{n-3}|^{2}\right).

And for
k=n-2
:

\displaystyle I_{3}=

\displaystyle-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{\gamma(w)\to\gamma(w_{n-3})}\left(\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w))+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(w_{n-3})|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to 0}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|w|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to w_{n-3}}\left(\varphi(w)+2\log\left|1-w_{n-3}\right|+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log\left|\frac{w-w_{n-3}}{1-w_{n-3}}\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to 0}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|w|^{2}\right).

Thus, we have:

I_{2}+I_{3}=-2h^{\prime}\log\left|1-w_{n-3}\right|.

Also for
k=n-1
:

\displaystyle I_{4}=

\displaystyle-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{\gamma(w)\to\gamma(1)}\left(\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w))+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(1)|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to 1}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|w-1|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to 1}\left(2\log\left|1-w_{n-3}\right|+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log\left|\frac{w-1}{1-w_{n-3}}\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to 1}\left(\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|w-1|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=-2h^{\prime}\log\left|1-w_{n-3}\right|.

And finally, for the contribution of infinity:

\displaystyle I_{5}=

\displaystyle-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{\gamma(w)\to\gamma(\infty)}\left(\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w))+\left(1+\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|\gamma(w)|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to\infty}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1+\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|w|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to\infty}\left(2\log\left|1-w_{n-3}\right|+\left(1+\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log\left|\frac{w-w_{n-3}}{1-w_{n-3}}\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to\infty}\left(\left(1+\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|w|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=2h^{\prime}\log\left|1-w_{n-3}\right|.

Thus, we have for all
j
:

\Delta\log\mathsf{H}=\sum_{i=1}^{5}I_{i}=-2\left(hs+h^{\prime}(s^{\prime}-2)\right)\log\left|1-w_{n-3}\right|=-2\log|f_{(\sigma_{j,j+1},\sigma^{\prime}_{n-3,n-2})}|,

(74)

where we used (
67
) in the last equality.

•

i=n-2
case:

Now, we look at the case with
i=n-2
and again denote the morphism by
\gamma
. We have
\gamma=1-w
in this case. Furthermore

\displaystyle\varphi(w)=\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w))+\log\left|\frac{\partial\gamma(w)}{\partial w}\right|^{2}=\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w)),

\displaystyle\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(w_{k})|=\left\{\begin{split}&\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(w_{k})|=\log\left|w-w_{k}\right|\>\>\>\>\>\>&k\leq n-3,\\
&\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(1)|=\log\left|1-w\right|\>\>\>\>\>\>&k=n-2,\\
&\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(0)|=\log\left|w\right|\>\>\>\>\>\>&k=n-1.\\
\end{split}\right.

Accordingly, it is quite clear that the variation is zero in this case, which is in agreement with the lemma.

•

i=n-1
case:

The last and perhaps the most subtle case is the case with
i=n-1
. Here we have
\gamma=w/(w-1)
, and

\displaystyle\varphi(w)=\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w))+\log\left|\frac{\partial\gamma(w)}{\partial w}\right|^{2}=\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w))-2\log\left|1-w\right|^{2},

\displaystyle\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(w_{k})|=\left\{\begin{split}&\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(w_{k})|=\log\left|\frac{w-w_{k}}{(1-w)(1-w_{k})}\right|\>\>\>\>\>\>&k\leq n-2,\\
&\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(\infty)|=\log\left|\frac{1}{1-w}\right|\>\>\>\>\>\>&k=n-1.\\
\end{split}\right.

Using these we again decompose (
71
) and write for
k\leq n-2
:

\displaystyle\tilde{I}_{1}=

\displaystyle-hm\sum_{k=1}^{s}\left(\lim_{\gamma(w)\to\gamma(w_{k})}\left(\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w))+\left(1-\frac{1}{m}\right)\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(w_{k})|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+hm\sum_{k=1}^{s}\left(\lim_{w\to w_{k}}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1-\frac{1}{m}\right)\log|w-w_{k}|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\sum_{k=s+1}^{n-2}\left(\lim_{\gamma(w)\to\gamma(w_{k})}\left(\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w))+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(w_{k})|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\sum_{k=s+1}^{n-2}\left(\lim_{w\to w_{k}}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|w-w_{k}|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=-hm\sum_{k=1}^{s}\left(\lim_{w\to w_{k}}\left(2\log\left|1-w\right|^{2}+\left(1-\frac{1}{m}\right)\log\left|\frac{w-w_{k}}{(1-w)(1-w_{k})}\right|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+hm\sum_{k=1}^{s}\left(\lim_{w\to w_{k}}\left(\left(1-\frac{1}{m}\right)\log|w-w_{k}|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\sum_{k=s+1}^{n-2}\left(\lim_{w\to w_{k}}\left(2\log\left|1-w\right|^{2}+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log\left|\frac{w-w_{k}}{(1-w)(1-w_{k})}\right|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\sum_{k=s+1}^{n-2}\left(\lim_{w\to w_{k}}\left(\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|w-w_{k}|^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=-2h\sum_{k=1}^{s}\log\left|1-w_{k}\right|^{2}-2h^{\prime}\sum_{k=s+1}^{n-2}\log\left|1-w_{k}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=-2h\sum_{k=1}^{s}\log\left|1-w_{k}\right|^{2}-2h^{\prime}\sum_{k=s+1}^{n-3}\log\left|1-w_{k}\right|^{2},

where in the last equality, we used the fact that
w_{n-2}=0
.
For
k=n-1
we have

\displaystyle\tilde{I}_{2}=

\displaystyle-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{\gamma(w)\to\gamma(\infty)}\left(\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w))+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|\gamma(w)-\gamma(\infty)|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to 1}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|1-w|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to\infty}\left(\varphi(w)+2\log\left|1-w\right|^{2}+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log\left|\frac{1}{1-w}\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to 1}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1-\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|1-w|^{2}\right),

and for the point at the infinity, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{I}_{3}=

\displaystyle-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{\gamma(w)\to\gamma(1)}\left(\tilde{\varphi}(\gamma(w))+\left(1+\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|\gamma(w)|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to\infty}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1+\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|w|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=-h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to 1}\left(\varphi(w)+2\log\left|1-w\right|^{2}+\left(1+\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log\left|\frac{w}{1-w}\right|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+h^{\prime}m^{\prime}\lim_{w\to\infty}\left(\varphi(w)+\left(1+\frac{1}{m^{\prime}}\right)\log|w|^{2}\right).

Thus we see that
\tilde{I}_{2}+\tilde{I}_{3}=0
and consequently again for all
j
:

\Delta\log\mathsf{H}=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\tilde{I}_{i}=-2h\sum_{k=1}^{s}\log\left|1-w_{k}\right|^{2}-2h^{\prime}\sum_{k=s+1}^{n-3}\log\left|1-w_{k}\right|^{2}=-2\log|f_{(\sigma_{j,j+1},\sigma^{\prime}_{n-1,n})}|,

(75)

where we again used (
67
) in the last equality.

By putting together all of the cases above, we see that

\Delta\log\mathsf{H}=\log\mathsf{H}\circ(\sigma_{j,j+1},\sigma^{\prime}_{i,i+1})-\log\mathsf{H}=-2\log|f_{(\sigma_{j,j+1},\sigma^{\prime}_{i,i+1})}|,\>\>\>\>\>\>\forall i,j.

(76)

Had we chosen any other way to fix
0,1
and
\infty
by finding the appropriate generators and 1-cocycles, the calculations analogous to the one above would yield the same result. In fact, the inclusion of
h_{k}m_{k}
’s in the definition of
\mathsf{H}
ensures that no matter how we choose to fix these points, this lemma holds. Finally, the case with more kinds of branch points can be derived inductively from the calculation above. Thus (
76
) holds in general, and it means that under the action of the elements of
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{s}}\right)
,
\mathsf{H}
transforms according to the rule
(\mathsf{H\circ\eta})|f_{\eta}|^{2}=\mathsf{H}
. This means that
\mathsf{H}
is a Hermitian metric in the holomorphic
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
\lambda_{0,\boldsymbol{m}}
over
\mathfrak{M}_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{m})
.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

When
O
is a punctured orbifold Riemann surface with signature
(0;m_{1},\dots,m_{n_{e}}

;n_{p}>3)
, the Hermitian metric
\mathsf{H}
on
\mathbb{Q}
-line bundle
\lambda_{0,\boldsymbol{m}}
is defined as (see
61
)

\mathsf{H}=\mathsf{h}_{1}^{m_{1}h_{1}}\dotsm\mathsf{h}_{n_{e}}^{m_{n_{e}}h_{n_{e}}}\mathsf{h}_{n_{e}+1}\dotsm\mathsf{h}_{n-1}\mathsf{h}_{n}^{-1}.

It is worth noting that the proof of Lemma
3.2
for the case of punctured Riemann surfaces can be found in
park2015potentials
and is just a matter of redoing the calculations above with the appropriate 1-cocycles.

The Poincaré metric on
\mathbb{H}
can be push-forwarded by the covering map
J:\mathbb{H}\to O
to obtain the hyperbolic metric
e^{\varphi(w)}|\differential{w}|^{2}
on orbifold Riemann surface
O
as follows,

e^{\varphi(w)}=\frac{\left|J^{-1}(w)^{\prime}\right|^{2}}{\left(\imaginary J^{-1}(w)\right)^{2}}.

(77)

The condition that the curvature is constant and equal to
-1
, except at singularities, means that the function
\varphi(w)
satisfies the Liouville’s equation

\partial_{w}\partial_{\bar{w}}\varphi=\frac{1}{2}e^{\varphi},

(78)

on
X_{O}^{\text{reg}}
. Moreover, as discussed in lemma
C.1
, one can derive the following asymptotic behavior for
\varphi(w)
near cusps and conical singularities:

\varphi(w)=\left\{\begin{split}&-2(1-\frac{1}{m_{i}})\log|w-w_{i}|+\log\frac{4|J^{(i)}_{1}|^{-\frac{2}{m_{i}}}}{m_{i}^{2}}+\text{\scriptsize{$\mathcal{O}$}}\left(1\right)\hskip 39.83368ptw\to w_{i},\\
&-2\log|w-w_{j}|-2\log\left|\log\left|\frac{w-w_{j}}{J^{(j)}_{1}}\right|\right|+\text{\scriptsize{$\mathcal{O}$}}\left(1\right)\hskip 56.9055ptw\to w_{j},\\
&-2\log|w|-2\log\log\left|\frac{w}{J^{(n)}_{-1}}\right|+\order{|w|^{-1}},\hskip 79.6678ptw\to\infty,\end{split}\right.

with
i=1,\dots,n_{e}
and
j=n_{e}+1,\dots,n-1
. Also, we have
42
42

42

The Liouville’s theorem in complex analysis is used.

\begin{split}\hskip 2.84526ptT_{\varphi}(w)=\partial_{w}^{2}\varphi-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{w}\varphi)^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\left(\frac{h_{i}}{2(w-w_{i})^{2}}+\frac{c_{i}}{w-w_{i}}\right),\end{split}

(79)

with
h_{i}=1
for
i=n_{e}+1,\dots,n-1
and

T_{\varphi}(w)=\frac{1}{2w^{2}}+\frac{c_{n}}{w^{3}}+\order{|w|^{-4}}\quad\text{as}\quad w\to\infty,

(80)

where

\left\{\begin{split}&c_{i}\equiv-\frac{h_{i}J^{(i)}_{2}}{\left(J^{(i)}_{1}\right)^{2}},\hskip 62.59596pti=1,\dots,n_{e},\\
&c_{j}\equiv-\frac{J^{(j)}_{2}}{\left(J^{(j)}_{1}\right)^{2}},\hskip 59.75095ptj=n_{e}+1,\dots,n-1,\\
&c_{n}\equiv J_{0}^{(j)},\hskip 88.2037ptj=n,\end{split}\right.

(81)

are the so-called
accessory parameters
of the Fuchsian differential equation. The accessory parameters
c_{k}=c_{k}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n-3})
for
k=1,\dots,n
, can be regarded as real-analytic functions on
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
.
43
43

43

This is a consequence of the fact that the Liouville filed
\varphi
is a real-analytic function on
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
.
In addition, the expansion of (
79
) around
w\rightarrow\infty
gives

T_{\varphi}(w)=\frac{1}{w}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}c_{k}+\frac{1}{w^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\left(\frac{h_{k}}{2}+c_{k}w_{k}\right)+\frac{1}{w^{3}}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}w_{k}(h_{k}+c_{k}w_{k})+\order{|w|^{-4}}\quad\text{as}\quad w\to\infty.

By equating the above expansion with (
80
), we get three conditions on the accessory parameters:

\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}c_{k}=0,\qquad\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(h_{k}+2c_{k}w_{k})=1,\qquad\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}w_{k}(h_{k}+c_{k}w_{k})=c_{n},

(82)

which enables us to express
c_{n-2}
,
c_{n-1}
, and
c_{n}
explicitly in terms of
w_{1},\dots,w_{n-1}
and the remaining
n-3
accessory parameters.

Consider the Riemann orbisurface
O\cong[\mathbb{H}/\penalty\Gamma]
as a base point in the Teichmüller space
\mathcal{T}_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{m})
. Moreover, consider the solution of the Beltrami equation (
30
)
such that the fixed points of
\kappa_{n-2},\kappa_{n-1},\kappa_{n}
are at
z_{n-2}=0
,
z_{n-1}=1
, and
z_{n}=\infty
. Then the generators
\kappa_{n-2}^{\mu},\kappa_{n-1}^{\mu},\kappa_{n}^{\mu}
of
\Gamma^{\mu}=f^{\mu}\circ\Gamma\circ(f^{\mu})^{-1}
will also have fixed points
0
,
1
, and
\infty
respectively. Accordingly, the Riemann orbisurface
O^{\mu}\cong[\mathbb{H}/\penalty\Gamma^{\mu}]
can be uniquely and complex-analytically embedded in
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
in such a way that the punctures on
O^{\mu}
corresponding to the elements
\kappa_{n-2}^{\mu}
,
\kappa_{n-1}^{\mu}
, and
\kappa_{n}^{\mu}
are mapped into
0
,
1
, and
\infty
. Denote by
J_{\mu}
the normalized covering map
J_{\mu}:\mathbb{H}\to O^{\mu}
corresponding to this embedding and let
w_{i}^{\mu}=\left(J_{\mu}\circ f^{\mu}\right)(z_{i})
. Then, the map
\Psi:\mathcal{T}_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{m})\to\mathbb{C}^{n-3}
, defined by

\Psi\circ\Phi(\mu)=(w_{1}^{\mu},\dots,w_{n-3}^{\mu})\in\mathbb{C}^{n-3},

(83)

is well defined, and its image in
\mathbb{C}^{n-3}
coincides with
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
. According to the above considerations, we have the following closed commutative diagram

\begin{CD}\mathbb{H}@>{f^{\mu}}>{}>\mathbb{H}\\
@V{J}V{}V@V{}V{J_{\mu}}V\\
O@>{F^{\mu}}>{}>O^{\mu}\end{CD}

(84)

where
F^{\mu}
is the mapping of
O
onto
O^{\mu}
. From the above commutative diagram, we can deduce that the
F^{\mu}
is a quasi-conformal homeomorphism of
\mathbb{C}
onto itself with

\partial_{\bar{w}}F^{\mu}=M\partial_{w}F^{\mu},

(85)

where

M\overset{\text{\tiny\it def.}}{\equiv}\left(\mu\circ J^{-1}\right)\frac{\overline{(J^{-1})^{\prime}}}{(J^{-1})^{\prime}}.

(86)

Consider the Beltrami differential
\varepsilon\mu
, where
\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{-1,1}(\mathbb{H},\Gamma)
and
\varepsilon
is a sufficiently small complex number. The function,
f^{\varepsilon\mu}(z)
is a real-analytic function of
\varepsilon
for each particular
z\in\mathbb{H}
. Let

\dot{f}_{+}^{\mu}\overset{\text{\tiny\it def.}}{\equiv}\left.\left(\partialderivative{\varepsilon}f^{\varepsilon\mu}\right)\right|_{\varepsilon=0},\qquad\dot{f}_{-}^{\mu}\overset{\text{\tiny\it def.}}{\equiv}\left.\left(\partialderivative{\bar{\varepsilon}}f^{\varepsilon\mu}\right)\right|_{\varepsilon=0};

(87)

then (see
(
Ahlfors_quasiconformal_06
, Section V.C)
)

\left\{\begin{split}&\dot{f}_{+}^{\mu}=-\frac{1}{\pi}\iint_{\mathbb{H}}\mu(z^{\prime})\,R(z^{\prime},z)\differential[2]{z^{\prime}},\\
&\dot{f}_{-}^{\mu}=-\frac{1}{\pi}\iint_{\mathbb{H}}\overline{\mu(z^{\prime})}\,R(\overline{z^{\prime}},z)\differential[2]{z^{\prime}},\end{split}\right.

(88)

where

R(z^{\prime},z)\overset{\text{\tiny\it def.}}{\equiv}\frac{1}{z^{\prime}-z}+\frac{z-1}{z^{\prime}}-\frac{z}{z^{\prime}-1}=\frac{z(z-1)}{(z^{\prime}-z)z^{\prime}(z^{\prime}-1)}.

(89)

In turn, the function
F^{\mu}(w)
is holomorphic with respect to
\varepsilon
for each particular
w\in\mathbb{C}
, and

\dot{F}^{\mu}(w)=-\frac{1}{\pi}\iint_{\mathbb{C}}M(w^{\prime})\,R(w^{\prime},w)\differential[2]{w^{\prime}},

(90)

where
\dot{F}^{\mu}
is understood to be given by
(\partial F^{\varepsilon\mu}/\partial\varepsilon)|_{\varepsilon=0}
and
M
was defined by Eq.(
86
).
44
44

44

According to (
85
),
\partial_{\bar{w}}\dot{F}=M
. The Green-function equation and solution for this equation are given by
\partial_{\bar{w}}R(w^{\prime},w)=-\pi\delta(w^{\prime},w)
and (
90
), respectively. Therefore, the kernel
R(w^{\prime},w)
, roughly speaking, inverts the action of
\bar{\partial}
-operator on Beltrami differentials on
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
. The precise statement (see
(
Takhtajan:2001uj
, Lemma 5)
and
(
Ahlfors_quasiconformal_06
, Section V.C)
) is essentially a version of the Pompeiu formula.
Proof of these assertions can be found in
Ahlfors_quasiconformal_06
;
Ahlfors1960RIEMANNSMT
;
Bers_1960
. Moreover, let

R_{i}(w)=-\frac{1}{\pi}R(w,w_{i})=-\frac{w_{i}(w_{i}-1)}{\pi(w-w_{i})w(w-1)},\qquad\qquad i=1,\dots,n-3;

(91)

where
R_{i}
s are linearly independent and generate the space
\mathcal{H}^{2,0}(O)
. Denote by
\{Q_{i}\}_{i=1,\dots,n-3}
, the basis in
\mathcal{H}^{2,0}(O)
biorthogonal to
\{R_{i}\}_{i=1,\dots,n-3}
in the sense of inner product (
317
) — i.e.
\langle R_{i},Q_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij}
where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker delta. The desired basis in
\mathcal{H}^{-1,1}(\mathbb{H},\Gamma)\cong T_{\Phi(0)}\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
has the form

\mu_{i}(z)=\rho(z)^{-1}\overline{q_{i}(z)},

(92)

where
q_{i}(z)=Q_{i}\circ J(z)\,J^{\prime}(z)^{2}
for
i=1,\dots,n-3
form a basis of the complex vector space
\mathcal{H}^{2,0}(\mathbb{H},\Gamma)\cong T_{\Phi(0)}^{\ast}\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
. These
q_{i}
s are biorthogonal to
r_{i}=R_{i}\circ J\,J^{\prime 2}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,0}(\mathbb{H},\Gamma)
with respect to the Petersson inner product.
45
45

45

In the space
\mathcal{H}^{2,0}(\mathbb{H},\Gamma)
, it is defined as
\langle q_{1},q_{2}\rangle=\iint_{\mathcal{F}(\Gamma)}q_{1}(z)\,\overline{q_{2}(z)}\,\rho^{-1}(z)\differential[2]{z}.
The basis
q_{i}^{{}^{{}_{\Phi(\mu)}}}\in\mathcal{H}^{2,0}(\mathbb{H},\Gamma^{\mu})
and
\mu_{i}^{{}^{{}_{\Phi(\mu)}}}\in\mathcal{H}^{-1,1}(\mathbb{H},\Gamma^{\mu})
for
\Phi(\mu)\in\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
can also be defined in a similar way. Then, the following lemma connects the motion of punctures and conical singularities on
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
with the geometry of Teichmüller space:

Lemma 3.3
.

For The mapping
\Psi:\mathcal{T}_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{m})\to\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
is a complex-analytic covering, and we have

\differential{\Psi_{\Phi(\mu)}}(\mu_{i}^{{}^{{}_{\Phi(\mu)}}})=\partialderivative{w^{\mu}_i}\quad\text{and}\quad\Psi_{\Phi(\mu)}^{\ast}(\differential{w^{\mu}_i})=\mathrm{r}_{i}^{{}^{{}_{\Phi(\mu)}}},\qquad i=1,\dots,n-3.

(93)

Proof.

Let us start with proving the statement
\differential{\Psi_{\Phi(\mu)}}(\mu_{i}^{{}^{{}_{\Phi(\mu)}}})=\partialderivative{w^{\mu}_i}
which is actually repeating the proof of Lemma 3 in
Zograf1988ONLE
;
Remark
3.1
implies that it is sufficient to verify this statement at the point
\Phi(0)
. In a neighborhood of
\Phi(0)\in\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
the Bers’ coordinates
(t_{1},\dots,t_{n-3})\in\mathbb{C}^{n-3}
are determined in the basis
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n-3}\in\mathcal{H}^{-1,1}(\mathbb{H},\Gamma)
from the expansion

\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{n-3}t_{i}\mu_{i},

(94)

where
\mu\in\mathcal{H}^{-1,1}(\mathbb{H},\Gamma)\cap\mathfrak{D}(\Gamma)
. In these coordinates, the mapping
\Psi
is given by

(t_{1},\dots,t_{n-3})\overset{\Psi}{\longmapsto}\left(F^{\mu}(w_{1}),\dots,F^{\mu}(w_{n-3})\right),

(95)

where
(w_{1},\dots,w_{n-3})=\Psi\circ\Phi(0)\in\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
. Since
F^{\mu}
depends complex-analytically on
t_{1},\dots,t_{n-3}
, the mapping
\Psi
is also complex-analytic. We now compute its differential
\differential{\Psi}
at the point
\Phi(0)\in\mathcal{T}(\Gamma)
: It follows from the definitions of
\mu_{i}
and
M_{i}
, equations (
92
) and (
86
), as well as Eq.(
77
) that

M_{i}=\mu_{i}\circ J^{-1}\frac{\overline{(J^{-1})^{\prime}}}{(J^{-1})^{\prime}}=\left(\imaginary J^{-1}\right)^{2}\overline{q_{i}\circ J^{-1}}\frac{\overline{(J^{-1})^{\prime}}}{(J^{-1})^{\prime}}=e^{-\varphi}\overline{Q_{i}},

(96)

where
Q_{i}=q_{i}\circ J^{-1}(J^{-1})^{\prime 2}
. Then, using the above equation, the definition of
R_{i}
s in (
91
), and Eq.(
90
), we get that

\begin{split}\dot{F}^{\mu_{i}}(w_{j})&=\iint_{\mathbb{C}}M_{i}(w)\,R_{j}(w)\differential[2]{w}\overset{\eqref{MiQi}}{=}\iint_{\mathbb{C}}e^{-\varphi}\overline{Q_{i}}\,R_{j}(w)\differential[2]{w}\overset{\eqref{Qinnerprod}}{=}\langle R_{j},Q_{i}\rangle=\delta_{ij}.\end{split}

(97)

This precisely means that in these coordinates the differential
\differential{\Psi}\big|_{\Phi(0)}
is given by the identity matrix — i.e.
\differential{\Psi}_{\Phi(0)}(\mu_{i})=\partialderivative{w_i}
. Additionally, the statement
\Psi_{\Phi(0)}^{\ast}(\differential{w_i})=\mathrm{r}_{i}
follows from
\differential{\Psi}_{\Phi(0)}(\mu_{i})=\partialderivative{w_i}
by observing that (1,0)-forms
\differential{w}_{i}
are dual to vector fields
\partialderivative{w_i}
and the quadratic differentials
r_{i}
are dual to harmonic Beltrami differentials
\mu_{i}
with respect to the Kodaira-Serre paring (
36
) — i.e.
(\mu_{i},r_{j})=\delta_{ij}
. Accordingly, it follows from Eq. (
93
) that the mapping
\Psi
is a local diffeomorphism. We now show that
\Psi
is a covering: For
\eta\in\operatorname{Aut}_{\ast}(\Gamma)
, denote by
z_{i}^{\eta}

(i=1,\dots,n_{e})
the fixed points of elliptic element
\eta(\tau_{i})\in\Gamma
of order
m_{i}
and by
z_{n_{e}+j}^{\eta}
the fixed points of the parabolic elements
\eta(\kappa_{j})\in\Gamma
for
j=1,\dots,n_{p}
. Let
\mathsf{s}_{\eta}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})\overset{\text{\tiny\it def.}}{\equiv}\left(J(z_{1}^{\eta}),\dots,J(z_{n}^{\eta})\right)
; the correspondence
\eta\mapsto\mathsf{s}_{\eta}
determines an epimorphism (i.e. a surjective group homomorphism) of the group
\operatorname{Mod}(\Gamma)
onto the product symmetric group
\operatorname{Symm}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathsf{s}}\right)
.
46
46

46

As a concrete example, consider the case of
n
-punctured sphere. In that case, the mapping class group
\operatorname{Mod}_{0,n}
is given by the braid group
B_{n}
quotiented by its center
farb2011primer
.
The kernel of this epimorphism is
\operatorname{Aut}(\Psi)

Zograf1988ONLE
. The mapping
\Psi
is invariant under
\operatorname{Aut}(\Psi)
and it is clear that
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}=\mathcal{T}_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{m})/\operatorname{Aut}(\Psi)
. Hence,
\Psi
is a covering with automorphism group
\operatorname{Aut}(\Psi)
.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

On
\mathcal{T}_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{m})
, each elliptic and cuspidal TZ metric
47
47

47

The
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\text{ell}}_{\text{TZ},i}
for
i=1,\dots,n_{e}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle^{\text{cusp}}_{\text{TZ},n_{e}+j}
for
j=1,\dots,n_{p}
.
remains unchanged under the automorphism group of the covering
\Psi:\mathcal{T}_{0,n}(\boldsymbol{m})\to\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
— i.e. the pure mapping classes
\operatorname{Mod}_{0}
. Furthermore, each metric establishes a Kähler metric on
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
.

Remark 3.6
.

We can also rewrite the expression for Energy-Momentum Tensor (
79
) using
R_{i}(w)
,

\text{Sch}\left(J^{-1};w\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}h_{i}\varE_{i}(w)-\pi\sum_{i=1}^{n-3}c_{i}R_{i}(w),

(98)

where

\left\{\begin{split}&\varE_{i}(w)=\frac{1}{2(w-w_{i})^{2}}-\frac{1}{2w(w-1)},\quad&i=1,\dots,n-1,\\
&\varE_{i}(w)=\frac{1}{2w(w-1)},\quad&i=n,\end{split}\right.

(99)

and
h_{i}=1
for
i=n_{e}+1,\dots,n
. Accordingly, one can also define the
e_{i}(z)=\varE_{i}\circ J\,J^{\prime 2}
on
\mathbb{H}
. These functions are actually the automorphic forms of weight four for
\Gamma
and they have non-vanishing constant terms at the singularities. Let us obtain the equation (
98
) for the simplest case with one branch point of order
m
at
w_{1}
and three punctures at
w_{2}=0,w_{3}=1,
and
w_{4}\rightarrow\infty
, respectively. Solving (
82
) in favor of
c_{3}
and
c_{2}
gives

c_{3}=-(c_{1}+c_{2}),\hskip 14.22636ptc_{2}=-\frac{1+h_{1}+2c_{1}(w_{1}-w_{3})}{2(w_{2}-w_{3})}.

By substituting the above relations in (
79
) and noting that
w_{2}=0,w_{3}=1
, one obtains

\displaystyle T_{\varphi}=h_{1}\left(\frac{1}{2(w-w_{1})^{2}}-\frac{1}{2w(w-1)}\right)+\frac{1}{2w^{2}}+\frac{1}{2(w-1)^{2}}-\frac{1}{2w(w-1)}

\displaystyle+c_{1}\left(\frac{1}{w-w_{1}}+\frac{w_{1}-1}{w}-\frac{w_{1}}{w-1}\right),

which by using (
99
) and (
91
) is equal to (
98
). The desired general case can be obtained in the same way.

Corollary 3.1
.

The following statements are true:

(i)

\dot{F}^{i}(0)=\dot{F}^{i}(1)=0
and

\dot{F}^{i}(w)=\text{\scriptsize{$\mathcal{O}$}}\left(|w|^{2}\right)\quad\text{as}\quad w\to\infty
.

(ii)

\partial_{\bar{w}}\dot{F}^{i}=M_{i}=e^{-\varphi}\overline{Q_{i}}
.

(iii)

The functions
\dot{F}^{i}(w)
have the following asymptotics

\hskip-14.22636pt\dot{F}^{i}(w)=\left\{\begin{split}&\delta_{ij}+(w-w_{j})\,\partial_{w}\dot{F}^{i}(w_{j})+\text{\scriptsize{$\mathcal{O}$}}\left(\frac{|w-w_{j}|}{\log|w-w_{j}|}\right)\hskip 2.84544pt(j=1,\dots,n_{e})\hskip 2.84544pt\text{as}\hskip 5.69046ptw\to w_{j},\\
&\delta_{ij}+(w-w_{j})\,\partial_{w}\dot{F}^{i}(w_{j})+\text{\scriptsize{$\mathcal{O}$}}\left(\frac{|w-w_{j}|}{\log|w-w_{j}|}\right)\hskip 2.84544pt(j=n_{e}+1,\dots,n-1)\hskip 2.84544pt\text{as}\hskip 5.69046ptw\to w_{j},\\
&w\,\partial_{w}\dot{F}^{i}(\infty)+\text{\scriptsize{$\mathcal{O}$}}\left(\frac{|w|}{\log|w|}\right)\hskip 56.9055pt\hskip 2.84544pt\text{as}\hskip 5.69046ptw\to\infty.\end{split}\right.

Proof.

The part
(i)
can be proved by using the equation (
90
) and the expression for
R(w,w_{i})
in (
91
). The part
(ii)
can also be proved by using (
90
) and (
96
). The general idea of proof the part
(iii)
can also be found in
(
park2015potentials
, Remark 3)
.
∎

In section
4
, we will need the derivatives of
\varphi
with respect to the variables
w_{1},\dots,w_{n-3}
and the following lemma will enable us to give a geometric description of them (see
(
Zograf1988ONLE
, Lemma 4)
):

Lemma 3.4
.

The Liouville filed
\varphi
is a continuously differentiable function on
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
, and

\partial_{w_{i}}\varphi+\dot{F}^{i}\partial_{w}\varphi+\partial_{w}\dot{F}^{i}=0,\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,\dots,n-3,

(100)

Proof.

It can be proved exactly in the same way as Lemma 4 in
Zograf1988ONLE
. Let
\Gamma
be a Fuchsian group of the first kind that uniformizes the orbifold Riemann surface
O
and let
f^{\mu}(z)
be the unique solution of the Beltrami equation (
30
) with
\mathcal{H}^{-1,1}(\mathbb{H},\Gamma)\ni\mu=t_{1}\,\mu_{1}+\dotsm+t_{n-3}\,\mu_{n-3}
that fixes the points
z_{n-2}=0
,
z_{n-1}=1
, and
z_{n}=\infty
. The function
F^{\mu}(w)=(J_{\mu}\circ f^{\mu}\circ J^{-1})(w)
is differentiable with respect to
w
on
O
and depends analytically on the Bers’ coordinates
t_{1},\dots,t_{n-3}
. It follows from the commutative diagram (
84
) that
J_{\mu}
and
J^{\prime}_{\mu}
are continuously differentiable with respect to the Bers’ coordinates
t_{1},\dots,t_{n-3}
and that suitable branches of the functions
J_{\mu}^{-1}
and
(J_{\mu}^{-1})^{\prime}
have this property locally outside the set of singular points. The continuous differentiability of
\varphi
on
\mathcal{M}_{0,n}
now follows from Eq.(
77
) and Lemma
3.3
. As for equation (
100
), it is a reformulation of lemma
3.1
due to Ahlfors
Ahlfors1961SomeRO
on the vanishing of the first variation of the area element in the Poincaré metric on
O
under quasi-conformal mappings corresponding to harmonic Beltrami differentials: for any
\mu\in\mathcal{H}^{-1,1}(\mathbb{H},\Gamma)

\mathcal{L}_{\mu}\rho=\partialderivative{\varepsilon}\Bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}(f^{\varepsilon\mu})^{\ast}(\rho)=\partialderivative{\varepsilon}\frac{|\partial_{z}f^{\varepsilon\mu}|^{2}}{\left(\imaginary f^{\varepsilon\mu}\right)^{2}}\Bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}=0.

(101)

Let
e^{\varphi^{\mu}(w)}\,|\differential{w}|^{2}
be the hyperbolic metric on the Riemann orbisurface
O^{\mu}
where
\varphi^{\mu}(w)=\varphi\left(w;F^{\mu}(w_{1}),\dots,F^{\mu}(w_{n-3})\right)
. From (
84
) one has

(f^{\mu})^{\ast}(\rho)=(f^{\mu})^{\ast}\underbrace{(J_{\mu})^{\ast}(e^{\varphi^{\mu}})}_{\rho}.

Then, using Eq.(
42
) as well as
F^{\mu}\circ J=J_{\mu}\circ f^{\mu}
, one gets

\frac{|\partial_{z}f^{\mu}|^{2}}{\left(\imaginary f^{\mu}\right)^{2}}=\exp(\varphi^{\mu} \circ J_{\mu} \circ f^{\mu})\left|\partial_{z}(J_{\mu}\circ f^{\mu})\right|^{2}=\exp(\varphi^{\mu} \circ F^{\mu} \circ J )\left|\partial_{w}F^{\mu}\circ J\right|^{2}|J^{\prime}|^{2}.

(102)

Finally, it follows from (
101
) that

\partialderivative{\varepsilon}\Bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}e^{\varphi^{\varepsilon\mu}\circ F^{\varepsilon\mu}\circ J}\left|\partial_{w}F^{\varepsilon\mu}\circ J\right|^{2}|J^{\prime}|^{2}=\partialderivative{\varphi^{\varepsilon\mu}}{\varepsilon}\Bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}+\partial_{w}\varphi\dot{F}^{\mu}+\partial_{w}\dot{F}^{\mu}=0,

(103)

which by setting
\mu=\mu_{i}
and recalling Lemma
3.3
(i.e.
d\Psi_{\Phi(0)}(\mu_{i})=\partial/\partial_{w_{i}}
) gives us our desired result (
100
).
∎

Corollary 3.2
.

According to Lemma
C.1
, the
\partial_{w_{i}}\varphi
has the following asymptotic expansion near the singular points

\partial_{w_{i}}\varphi(w)=\left\{\begin{split}&-\delta_{ij}\partial_{w}\varphi(w)-\big((w-w_{j})\partial_{w}\varphi(w)+1\big)\partial_{w}\dot{F}^{i}(w)+\text{\scriptsize{$\mathcal{O}$}}\left(1\right)\quad&\text{as}\quad&w\to w_{j\neq n},\\
&-\big(w\partial_{w}\varphi(w)+1\big)\partial_{w}\dot{F}^{i}(w)+\text{\scriptsize{$\mathcal{O}$}}\left(1\right)\quad&\text{as}\quad&w\to w_{n}=\infty.\end{split}\right.

Proof.

It follows from (
100
)
and from the asymptotics of
\dot{F}^{i}
(see Corollary
3.1
).
∎

Remark 3.7
.

One can also use the results of Ahlfors and Wolpert mentioned in subsection
3.1.2
, i.e. lemma
3.1
and Eq.(
45
), to calculate the variations of
\exp(\varphi^{\varepsilon\mu}(w))
on the orbifold Riemann surfaces
O^{\varepsilon\mu}=F^{\varepsilon\mu}(O)
. To do so, let us use the commutative diagram (
84
) once again to write

(F^{\mu})^{\ast}(e^{\varphi^{\mu}})=(J^{-1})^{\ast}(f^{\mu})^{\ast}(\rho).

(104)

Then, it is easy to show that the variations of hyperbolic metrics on Riemann orbisurfaces
O^{\varepsilon\mu}
are given by the same formulas in subsection
3.1.2
but with
\rho
replaced by
e^{\varphi}
and
f_{\mu\overline{\tilde{\mu}}}
replaced by
(J^{-1})^{\ast}(f_{\mu\overline{\tilde{\mu}}})=f_{\mu\overline{\tilde{\mu}}}\circ J^{-1}
. Moreover, for any
\mathtt{a}\in\mathbb{R}
we have

(F^{\mu})^{\ast}(e^{\mathtt{a}\varphi^{\mu}})=\left((F^{\mu})^{\ast}(e^{\varphi^{\mu}})\right)^{\mathtt{a}}.

(105)

Therefore, we get the following formulas for the first and second variations of
\exp(\varphi^{\varepsilon\mu}(w))

\left\{\begin{split}&\partialderivative{\varepsilon}\Bigg|_{\varepsilon=0}(F^{\varepsilon\mu})^{\ast}(e^{\mathtt{a}\varphi^{\varepsilon\mu}})=0,\\
\\
&\partialderivative{}{\varepsilon_1}{\bar{\varepsilon_2}}\Bigg|_{\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{2}=0}(F^{\varepsilon_{1}\mu+\varepsilon_{2}\tilde{\mu}})^{\ast}(e^{\mathtt{a}\varphi^{\varepsilon\mu}})=\frac{\mathtt{a}}{2}e^{\mathtt{a}\varphi}f_{\mu\overline{\tilde{\mu}}}\circ J^{-1}.\end{split}\right.

(106)

3.3
Schottky Space
\mathfrak{S}_{g,n}(\boldsymbol{m})

Let us start this subsection by recalling with more detail how a compact Riemann surface
X
of genus
g\geq 2
is uniformized by a Schottky group. We begin with a few well-known definitions. Schottky groups are an important class of
Kleinian groups
: discrete subgroups of Möbius group
\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
that act properly discontinuous on some domain (called region of discontinuity) of the Riemann sphere
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
. A
Schottky group

\Sigma
is strictly loxodromic Kleinian group which is also free and finitely generated
Maskit_1967
. If we denote its limit set by
\Lambda
(which is a Cantor set)
48
48

48

For more details on geometry of limit sets see Ref.
Seade_2015
.
then the
region of discontinuity

\Omega=\hat{\mathbb{C}}\backslash\Lambda
would be connected. Moreover, a Schottky group
\Sigma
of rank
g
will be called
marked
if we choose a relation-free system of generators
L_{1},\dots,L_{g}\in\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
. There is also a notion of equivalence between two marked Schottky groups:
(\Sigma;L_{1},\dots,L_{g})
is equivalent to
(\tilde{\Sigma};\tilde{L}_{1},\dots,\tilde{L}_{g})
if there exists a Möbius transformation
\varsigma\in\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
such that
\tilde{L}_{i}=\varsigma L_{i}\varsigma^{-1}
for all
i=1,\dots,g
. The set of equivalence classes of marked Schottky groups of genus
g
is called the
Schottky space
of genus
g
and is denoted by
\mathfrak{S}_{g}
. Similar to Fuchsian groups, Schottky groups can be used to construct surfaces since the action of
\Sigma
on
\Omega
produces a
compact
Riemann surface
\Omega/\penalty\Sigma
. An important result is that for every marked Schottky group
(\Sigma;L_{1},\dots,L_{g})
there is a
fundamental domain

\mathcal{D}
49
49

49

However, this fundamental domain
\mathcal{D}
is
not uniquely determined
by the choice of marking for the Schottky group
\Sigma
.
for
\Sigma
in
\Omega
. This domain is a (connected) region in
\hat{\mathbb{C}}
and it is bounded by
2g
disjoint Jordan curves
C_{1},\dots,C_{g},C^{\prime}_{1},\dots,C^{\prime}_{g}
with
C^{\prime}_{i}=-L_{i}(C_{i})
,
i=1,\dots,g
. The orientations of
C_{i}
and
C^{\prime}_{i}
are opposite and related to components of
\partial\mathcal{D}
. The standard form for representation of each
L_{i}
is

\bigg(L_{i}(w)-\tilde{a}_{i}\bigg)(w-\tilde{b}_{i})=\lambda_{i}\hskip 2.84526pt\bigg(L_{i}(w)-\tilde{b}_{i}\bigg)(w-\tilde{a}_{i}),\qquad w\in\hat{\mathbb{C}},

(107)

where
\tilde{a}_{i}
and
\tilde{b}_{i}
are the respective
attracting
and
repelling
fixed points of the loxodromic element
L_{i}
and
0<|\lambda_{i}|<1
is the corresponding multiplier. Given this normal form, one can explicitly construct the fundamental domain
\mathcal{D}
in the following way:
50
50

50

For more details, see
(
Gaberdiel:2010jf
, Appendix C)
.
Let us define the Möbius transformations

\varsigma_{\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{b}_{i}}(w)=\frac{\tilde{b}_{i}w+\tilde{a}_{i}}{w+1},

(108)

satisfying
\varsigma_{\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{b}_{i}}(0)=\tilde{a}_{i}
and
\varsigma_{\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{b}_{i}}(\infty)=\tilde{b}_{i}
, so that the generators
L_{i}
of the marked Schottky group
(\Sigma;L_{1},\dots,L_{g})
can be written as

L_{i}=\varsigma_{\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{b}_{i}}\hskip 2.84526pt\varsigma_{\lambda_{i}}\hskip 2.84526pt\varsigma_{\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{b}_{i}}^{-1}\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,\dots,g.

(109)

In the above equation, the Möbius transformation
\varsigma_{\lambda_{i}}
is defined by
\varsigma_{\lambda_{i}}(w)=\lambda_{i}w
. Then, a fundamental domain for
(\Sigma;L_{1},\dots,L_{g})
is given by

\mathcal{D}\overset{\text{\tiny\it def.}}{\equiv}\hat{\mathbb{C}}\Big\backslash\bigcup_{i=1}^{g}(\mathrm{D}_{i}\cup\mathrm{D}_{-i}),

(110)

where

\left\{\begin{split}&\mathrm{D}_{i}=\left\{w\in\mathbb{C}\,\Bigg|\,\frac{|w-\tilde{a}_{i}|}{|w-\tilde{b}_{i}|}<|\mathrm{R}_{i}|\right\}=\varsigma_{\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{b}_{i}}\hskip 2.84526pt\varsigma_{\mathrm{R}_{i}}(\mathrm{D}),\\
\\
&\mathrm{D}_{-i}=\left\{w\in\mathbb{C}\,\Bigg|\,\frac{|w-\tilde{b}_{i}|}{|w-\tilde{a}_{i}|}<|\mathrm{R}_{-i}|\right\}=\varsigma_{\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{b}_{i}}\hskip 2.84526pt\varsigma_{inv}\hskip 2.84526pt\varsigma_{\mathrm{R}_{-i}}(\mathrm{D}),\end{split}\right.

(111)

\varsigma_{inv}
is defined by
\varsigma_{inv}(w)=-1/w
, and
\mathrm{D}
is the unit disk,

\mathrm{D}=\left\{w\in\mathbb{C}\,\Big|\,|w|<1\right\}.

(112)

Here
\mathrm{R}_{i}
and
\mathrm{R}_{-i}
represent the radii of disks
\mathrm{D}_{i}
and
\mathrm{D}_{-i}
respectively and satisfy
51
51

51

Equation (
113
) makes clear the fact that, as mentioned in footnote
49
, the fundamental domain
\mathcal{D}
cannot be uniquely determined by a choice of marking for the Schottky group
\Sigma
.

\mathrm{R}_{i}\mathrm{R}_{-i}=\lambda_{i}\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,\dots,g.

(113)

The boundary
\partial\mathcal{D}=\bigcup_{i}C_{i}\cup C^{\prime}_{i}
has components

C_{i}=\varsigma_{\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{b}_{i}}\hskip 2.84526pt\varsigma_{\mathrm{R}_{i}}(C)\quad\text{and}\quad C^{\prime}_{i}=\varsigma_{\tilde{a}_{i},\tilde{b}_{i}}\hskip 2.84526pt\varsigma_{inv}\hskip 2.84526pt\varsigma_{\mathrm{R}_{-i}}(C),

(114)

where
C=\partial\mathrm{D}
is the unit circle. In the rest of this article, we will consistently presume that a marked Schottky group is
normalized
. This means that
\tilde{a}_{1}
equals 0,
\tilde{b}_{1}
is infinity, and
\tilde{a}_{2}
is 1. In particular, this means that
\infty\notin\mathcal{D}
.

Under the canonical holomorphic map
\Omega\to\Omega/\penalty\Sigma
, the boundary curves of a standard fundamental domain described above are mapped onto smooth non-intersecting simple closed curves
\mathsf{a}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{a}_{g}
on the Riemann surface. This motivates the following terminology introduced by Bers
Bers1961UniformizationBB
: A complete set of
retrosections
on a Riemann surface of genus
g
is a choice of
g
smooth simple non-intersecting, homologically independent, closed curves
\mathsf{a}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{a}_{g}
. We therefore see that a marked Schottky group, together with the choice of a standard fundamental domain, determines a Riemann surface with a complete set of retrosections. A much more profound statement is that
every
compact Riemann surface can be obtained in this way, which is the content of the classical Koebe’s retrosection theorem
Kra_1972
:

Theorem

(Koebe)
.

For every compact Riemann surface
X
of genus
g
with a complete set of retrosections
(\mathsf{a}_{1},\dots,\mathsf{a}_{g})
, there exists a marked Schottky group of genus
g
,
(\Sigma;L_{1},\dots,L_{g})
, and a fundamental domain
\mathcal{D}\subset\Omega
for
\Sigma
with
2g
boundary curves
C_{1},\dots,C_{g},C^{\prime}_{1},\dots,C^{\prime}_{g}
such that
X=\Omega/\penalty\Sigma
and the map
\Omega\to X
sends both
C_{i}
and
C^{\prime}_{i}
to
\mathsf{a}_{i}
. Moreover, the marked Schottky group is unique up to equivalence
(\Sigma;L_{1},\dots,L_{g})\sim(\tilde{\Sigma};\tilde{L}_{1},\dots,\tilde{L}_{g})
defined before as well as
L_{i}\mapsto L_{i}^{-1}
.

Remark 3.8
.

The above theorem implies that given a compact Riemann surface
X
uniformized by the marked Schottky group
(\Sigma;L_{1},\dots,L_{g})
, we can take the homology classes of
C_{1},\dots,C_{g}
as the generators
[\mathsf{a}_{1}],\dots,[\mathsf{a}_{g}]
in the symplectic basis of the first homology group
H_{1}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. However, determining a canonical basis for
H_{1}(X,\mathbb{Z})
, i.e. a symplectic basis
\left\{[\mathsf{a}_{1}],\dots,[\mathsf{a}_{g}],[\mathsf{b}_{1}],\dots,[\mathsf{b}_{g}]\right\}
with intersection pairings given by
\#\left([\mathsf{a}_{i}],[\mathsf{a}_{j}]\right)=0=\#\left([\mathsf{b}_{i}],[\mathsf{b}_{j}]\right)
and
\#\left([\mathsf{a}_{i}],[\mathsf{b}_{j}]\right)=\delta_{ij}
, depends also on the choice of
\mathsf{b}
-cycles on the Riemann surface
X
. Therefore, we can choose the elements
[\mathsf{b}_{1}],\dots,[\mathsf{b}_{g}]
in the canonical basis of
H_{1}(X,\mathbb{Z})
such that the projections of their representative curves onto the marked Schottky group
\Sigma
are precisely the marked generators
L_{1},\dots,L_{g}
.

Remark 3.9
.

The association between the set of normalized marked Schottky groups and the Schottky space
\mathfrak{S}_{g}
, found within
\mathbb{C}^{3g-3}
, is evidently bijective.
52
52

52

The space
\mathfrak{S}_{g}
is a finite covering of the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{g}
of compact Riemann surfaces.

For our purposes in the following sections, it will be crucial to give another (equivalent) definition for the Schottky space
\mathfrak{S}_{g}
. Let
\mathcal{A}^{-1,1}(\Omega,\Sigma)
be the complex Banach space of Beltrami differentials for
\Sigma
and, in analogy with the Teichmüller case, let us define the deformation space
\mathfrak{D}(\Sigma)
to be the open ball of radius
1
(in the sense of
L^{\infty}
-norm) in
\mathcal{A}^{-1,1}(\Omega,\Sigma)
:

\mathfrak{D}(\Sigma)=\left\{\mu\in\mathcal{A}^{-1,1}(\Omega,\Sigma)\,\big|\,\norm{\mu}_{\infty}<1\right\}.

(115)

A homeomorphism
F
of a plane domain
\Omega
onto another plane domain
\tilde{\Omega}
is said to be
quasi-conformal
if it satisfies the Beltrami equation at each point in
\Omega
. For each
\mu\in\mathfrak{D}(\Sigma)
, let
F^{\mu}
be the unique normalized (i.e.
F^{\mu}(0)=0
and
F^{\mu}(1)=1
) solution of the corresponding Beltrami equation on
\mathbb{C}
that gives a quasi-conformal homeomorphism of
\mathbb{C}
onto itself. Then, the restriction of
F^{\mu}
to the region of discontinuity
\Omega\subset\mathbb{C}
gives the desired quasi-conformal homeomorphism
F^{\mu}:\Omega\to\Omega^{\mu}
and each element
\mu\in\mathfrak{D}(\Sigma)
gives a faithful representation
\varrho_{\mu}
of
\Sigma
in
\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
according to the formula
\sigma\mapsto F^{\mu}\circ\sigma\circ(F^{\mu})^{-1}
,
\sigma\in\Sigma
. As mentioned before, two representations
\varrho_{\mu_{1}}
and
\varrho_{\mu_{2}}
are equivalent if they differ by an
inner automorphism
of
\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
, i.e., if
\varrho_{\mu_{2}}=\varsigma\varrho_{\mu_{1}}\varsigma^{-1}
,
\varsigma\in\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
. Accordingly, the Schottky space
\mathfrak{S}_{g}
is defined to be the set of equivalence classes of representations
\left[\varrho_{\mu}\right]:\Sigma\to\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})
,
\mu\in\mathfrak{D}(\Sigma)
. In other words,

\mathfrak{S}_{g}\cong\mathfrak{D}(\Sigma)/\sim,

(116)

where
\mu_{1}\sim\mu_{2}
if and only if
F^{\mu_{1}}\circ\sigma\circ(F^{\mu_{1}})^{-1}=F^{\mu_{2}}\circ\sigma\circ(F^{\mu_{2}})^{-1}
for all
\sigma\in\Sigma
(or equivalently,
F^{\mu_{1}}\big|_{\Lambda}=F^{\mu_{2}}\big|_{\Lambda}
). At
\mu=0
one recovers the group
\Sigma
which corresponds to the base point of
\mathfrak{S}_{g}
.

The above alternative definition of Schottky space
\mathfrak{S}_{g}
gives us the opportunity to define also the generalized Schottky space
\mathfrak{S}_{g,n}(\boldsymbol{m})
for Riemann orbisurfaces with signature
(g;m_{1},\dots,m_{n_{e}};n_{p})
. Let us consider the configuration spaces
\mathscr{F}_{n}\left(\Omega^{\mu}/\penalty\Sigma^{\mu}\right)=\mathscr{F}_{n}\left(\mathcal{D}^{\mu}\right)
with
\Sigma^{\mu}=F^{\mu}\circ\Sigma\circ(F^{\mu})^{-1},\hskip 2.84526pt\Omega^{\mu}=F^{\mu}(\Omega)
and
the deformation space of a marked Schottky group
(\Sigma;L_{1},\dots,L_{g})
together with a point
(w_{1},\dots,w_{n_{e}},w_{n_{e}+1},\dots,w_{n})\in\mathscr{F}_{n}\left(\mathcal{D}\right)
,

\mathfrak{D}(\Sigma;L_{1},\dots,L_{g};w_{1},\dots,w_{n_{e}};w_{n_{e}+1},\dots,w_{n})\\
=\left\{(\mu;w_{1}^{\mu},\dots,w_{n_{e}}^{\mu};w_{n_{e}+1}^{\mu},\dots,w_{n}^{\mu})\in\mathcal{A}^{-1,1}(\Omega,\Sigma)\times\mathscr{F}_{n}\left(\mathcal{D}^{\mu}\right)\,\Big|\,\norm{\mu}_{\infty}<1\right\},

(117)

where
w^{\mu}_{i}=F^{\mu}(w_{i})
. Just as in the case of
\mathfrak{S}_{g}
, each element
\mu\in\mathfrak{D}(\Sigma;L_{1},\dots,L_{g};w_{1},\dots,w_{n})
gives a faithful representation
\varrho_{\mu}
of
(\Sigma;L_{1},\dots,L_{g};w_{1},\dots,w_{n})
in
\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{C})\times\mathscr{F}_{n}\left(\mathcal{D}^{\mu}\right)
according to the formula
L_{i}\mapsto F^{\mu}\circ L_{i}\circ(F^{\mu})^{-1}
for all marked generators
L_{i}\in\Sigma
,
i=1,\dots,g
and
w_{j}\mapsto F^{\mu}(w_{j})
for
j=1,\dots,n
. Two representations are then equivalent,
\varrho_{\mu_{1}}\sim\varrho_{\mu_{2}}
, if
F^{\mu_{1}}\circ L_{i}\circ(F^{\mu_{1}})^{-1}=F^{\mu_{2}}\circ L_{i}\circ(F^{\mu_{2}})^{-1}
for all
i=1,\dots,g
as well as
w_{j}^{\mu_{1}}=w_{j}^{\mu_{2}}
for all
j=1,\dots,n
and the Schottky space
\mathfrak{S}_{g,n}(\boldsymbol{m})
is defined to be the set of equivalence classes of representations
\left[\varrho_{\mu}\right]

\mathfrak{S}_{g,n}(\boldsymbol{m})\cong\mathfrak{D}(\Sigma;L_{1},\dots,L_{g};w_{1},\dots,w_{n_{e}};w_{n_{e}+1},\dots,w_{n})/\sim.

(118)

Let us remind that the Schottky uniformization of an orbisurface
O
is connected with the Fuchsian uniformization of it by the commutative diagram (
13
). Accordingly, each marked Fuchsian group
\Gamma
with signature
(g;m_{1},\dots,m_{n_{e}},n_{p})
corresponds to the unique marked normalized Schottky group
\Sigma\simeq\Gamma/N
with the domain of discontinuity
\overset{{}_{\curlywedge}}{\Omega}
such that
\mathbb{H}/\penalty\Gamma\cong\overset{{}_{\curlywedge}}{\Omega}/\penalty\Sigma
and this correspondence determines the following map

\pi:\mathcal{T}_{g,n}(\boldsymbol{m})\to\mathfrak{S}_{g,n}(\boldsymbol{m})

(119)

by putting
w_{i}=J(z_{i})
for
i=1,\dots,n
.
53
53

53

This map has the same role as the covering map
\Psi
in lemma
3.3
and is a complex covering map.
We can use this map to understand the tangent and cotangent space to the Schottky space
\mathfrak{S}_{g,n}(\boldsymbol{m})
: Elements of
\mathcal{H}^{2,0}(\mathbb{H},\Gamma)
descend to meromorphic quadratic differentials for
\Sigma
— i.e. automorphic forms of weight 4 for
\Sigma
with simple poles at singular points of
\overset{{}_{\curlywedge}}{\Omega}
. The space of meromorphic quadratic differentials for
\Sigma
will be denoted by
\mathcal{H}^{2,0}(\overset{{}_{\curlywedge}}{\Omega},\Sigma)
and each
Q\in\mathcal{H}^{2,0}(\overset{{}_{\curlywedge}}{\Omega},\Sigma)
has the form

Q(w)=(q\circ J^{-1})(w)\left(J^{-1}(w)^{\prime}\right)^{2},\qquad q\in\mathcal{H}^{2,0}(\mathbb{H},\Gamma).

(120)

The vector space
\mathcal{H}^{2,0}(\overset{{}_{\curlywedge}}{\Omega},\Sigma)
coincides with the holomorphic cotangent space
T_{\pi\circ\Phi(0)}^{\ast}\mathfrak{S}_{g,n}(\boldsymbol{m})
to
\mathfrak{S}_{g,n}(\boldsymbol{m})
at the origin. This implies that the holomorphic tangent space
T_{\pi\circ\Phi(0)}\mathfrak{S}_{g,n}(\boldsymbol{m})
is identified with the complex vector space
\mathcal{H}^{-1,1}(\overset{{}_{\curlywedge}}{\Omega},\Sigma)
of harmonic Beltrami differentials.
54
54

54

Harmonic with respect to the hyperbolic metric on
\overset{{}_{\curlywedge}}{\Omega}
.
Each
M\in\mathcal{H}^{-1,1}(\overset{{}_{\curlywedge}}{\Omega},\Sigma)
has the form

M(w)=e^{-\varphi(w)}\overline{Q(w)},\qquad Q\in\mathcal{H}^{2,0}(\overset{{}_{\curlywedge}}{\Omega},\Sigma).

(121)

From Eq.(
117
), which implies that there exists a fibration
\jmath:\mathfrak{S}_{g,n}(\boldsymbol{m})\to\mathfrak{S}_{g}
whose fibers over the points
\pi\circ\Phi(\mu)\in\mathfrak{S}_{g}
are
\mathscr{F}_{n}\left(\Omega^{\mu}/\penalty\Sigma^{\mu}\right)
, it follows that
T_{\pi\circ\Phi(0)}^{\ast}\mathfrak{S}_{g,n}(\boldsymbol{m})
has a subspace
\jmath^{\ast}(T_{\pi\circ\Phi(0)}^{\ast}\mathfrak{S}_{g})\cong\mathcal{H}^{2,0}(\Omega,\Sigma)
. The standard basis in this subspace of
\mathcal{H}^{2,0}(\Omega,\Sigma)
is given by the following holomorphic automorphic forms of weight four for Schottky group,

P_{1}(w),\dots,P_{3g-3}(w)\in\mathcal{H}^{2,0}(\Omega,\Sigma).

(122)

Moreover, the
P_{i}
s actually coincide with the following cotangent vectors

\differential{\lambda_1},\dots,\differential{\lambda_g},\differential{a_3},\dots,\differential{a_g},\differential{b_2},\dots,\differential{b_g}\in\jmath^{\ast}(T_{\pi\circ\Phi(0)}^{\ast}\mathfrak{S}_{g}).

(123)

The subspace that is isomorphic to
T_{\pi\circ\Phi(0)}^{\ast}\mathscr{F}_{n}\left(\mathcal{D}\right)
corresponds to the complement of

\jmath^{\ast}(T_{\pi\circ\Phi(0)}^{\ast}

\mathfrak{S}_{g})
in
